1663pub async fn authorize<C: Connection + Clone>(
1664    http_req: HttpRequest,
1665    maybe_user: MaybeAuthenticatedUser,
1666    // W3: a `Result` rather than a plain extractor so that the **order** of
1667    // today's refusals is preserved. `AuthenticatedUser` used to be the first
1668    // extractor on this handler, so an unauthenticated request with an
1669    // unparseable query was answered 401 and never reached the body.
1670    // `MaybeAuthenticatedUser` cannot fail, which would have promoted the query
1671    // error to first place and turned that 401 into a 400 — a behaviour change
1672    // for nobody's benefit. Resolving both here keeps the precedence explicit.
1673    query: Result<web::Query<AuthorizeQuery>, actix_web::Error>,
1674    state: web::Data<AppState<C>>,
1675) -> HttpResponse {
1676    use actix_web::ResponseError;
1677
1678    let q = match query {
1679        Ok(q) => q.into_inner(),
1680        Err(query_error) => {
1681            return match maybe_user.into_result() {
1682                Err(auth_error) => auth_error.error_response(),
1683                Ok(_) => query_error.error_response(),
1684            };
1685        }
1686    };
1687
1688    let user = match resolve_authorize_principal(&state, &http_req, &q, maybe_user).await {
1689        Ok(principal) => principal,
1690        Err(response) => return *response,
1691    };
1692
1693    // T21.5 — a `client_id` that is a URL may be a client this tenant has
1694    // never seen. Here, before anything looks the client up, so that everything
1695    // below acts on an ordinary row: two `starts_with` calls for every
1696    // authorization request AXIAM has ever served, and a fetch only for a
1697    // tenant that has enabled the mechanism. It returns nothing and cannot
1698    // refuse the request — see `crate::cimd`.
1699    crate::cimd::materialise_if_cimd(&state, &http_req, user.tenant_id, &q.client_id).await;
1700
1701    // X7 G12 (plan §4.10). Classify request objects FIRST, before the PAR
1702    // branch below: a `request_uri` that is not a PAR handle would otherwise
1703    // fall into `par_service.consume` and come back as a generic
1704    // `invalid_request`, losing the code OIDC Core §3.1.2.6 defines and a
1705    // conformance suite matches on.
1706    //
1707    // The marker travels on the `AuthorizeRequest` rather than being answered
1708    // here so that the client and its `redirect_uri` are validated first: a
1709    // refusal is redirected only to a URI the client actually registered, and
1710    // otherwise answered directly.
1711    let request_object = classify_request_object(q.request.as_deref(), q.request_uri.as_deref());
1712
1713    // X7.2 — resolved once, for both carriers: the evidence describes the
1714    // session this request arrives in, which is the same session whether the
1715    // parameters came inline or through PAR.
1716    let session_evidence = resolve_session_evidence(&state, user.tenant_id, user.session_id).await;
1717
1718    // G-5 (D-53 (1)) — what this request's OP session achieved, captured before
1719    // the evidence moves into the request. `None` without a readable session:
1720    // nothing is then known about how the user authenticated, so nothing is
1721    // remembered or told. The return leg that arrives with a new session of the
1722    // user a step-up was recorded for tells SSF receivers the level changed.
1723    let session_acr = session_evidence
1724        .auth_time
1725        .map(|_| axiam_oauth2::acr::acr_for(&session_evidence.amr));
1726    // Whether this is a login hop's return leg. The step-up record is consumed
1727    // only once the authorization service has accepted the request — client,
1728    // `redirect_uri` and the rest (F4 W4 P23W4-02, T-404) — never on the marker
1729    // alone, which any page can put on a link.
1730    let return_leg = axiam_oauth2::login_hop::is_return_leg(q.login_hop.as_deref());
1731
1732    // B5. The pushed copy wins; the query string's copies are IGNORED, not
1733    // merged and not refused.
1734    //
1735    // The distinction is the whole of RFC 9101 §6.3, which RFC 9126 §4 adopts
1736    // by reference ("build an authorization request as defined in [RFC9101]"):
1737    //
1738    //   The authorization server MUST extract the set of authorization request
1739    //   parameters from the Request Object value. The authorization server MUST
1740    //   only use the parameters in the Request Object, even if the same
1741    //   parameter is provided in the query parameter.
1742    //
1743    // and §5 says in terms that a client MAY send them duplicated. So a
1744    // duplicated `response_type`/`redirect_uri`/`scope`/`code_challenge` is a
1745    // conformant request, and an earlier revision of this handler refused it —
1746    // which failed every FAPI 2.0 authorization module, since the OIDF suite
1747    // sends exactly that shape.
1748    //
1749    // The security argument the refusal was built on is sound and is satisfied
1750    // by ignoring rather than refusing: parameter confusion needs the inline
1751    // value to be *read* by something, and nothing below reads it. Every field
1752    // of the request comes from `params`, the pushed copy — as `state` and
1753    // `nonce` already did, for exactly this reason. `client_id` is the one
1754    // parameter that is still compared rather than ignored, because §6.3
1755    // requires the two to be identical; `par_service.consume` does that by
1756    // scoping the handle to the client that pushed it.
1757    //
1758    // A refused request object takes the inline branch whatever it carried:
1759    // there is nothing to consume, and the branch exists only to reach the
1760    // validation that decides how the refusal is reported.
1761    let request_uri = match request_object {
1762        Some(_) => None,
1763        None => q.request_uri,
1764    };
1765    let req = match request_uri {
1766        Some(request_uri) => {
1767            let params = match state
1768                .oauth2
1769                .par_service
1770                .consume(user.tenant_id, &q.client_id, &request_uri)
1771                .await
1772            {
1773                Ok(params) => params,
1774                // W3 (plan §4.0, F10). A pushed request lives 60 seconds. A
1775                // user who took longer than that to type a password comes back
1776                // here to a handle that no longer exists — and telling them
1777                // `invalid_request: request_uri is unknown, expired, or used`
1778                // describes a client bug that did not happen. On a return leg,
1779                // and only there, the recoverable code is used instead: the
1780                // relying party pushes again and restarts, which is RFC 9126
1781                // §2.2's own design rather than a workaround for it.
1782                //
1783                // Narrow on purpose. Only the "gone" refusal is remapped, only
1784                // when the marker says this request came back from the login
1785                // page, so an ordinary authorization request with a dead handle
1786                // gets exactly the answer it got before this wave.
1787                Err(e)
1788                    if axiam_oauth2::login_hop::is_return_leg(q.login_hop.as_deref())
1789                        && axiam_oauth2::par::is_request_uri_gone(&e) =>
1790                {
1791                    return authorize_error_response(
1792                        &http_req,
1793                        &OAuth2Error::InvalidRequestUri(
1794                            "the pushed authorization request expired while signing in \
1795                         (a request_uri lives 60 seconds); push it again and restart \
1796                         the authorization request"
1797                                .into(),
1798                        ),
1799                    );
1800                }
1801                // Every other `request_uri` refusal — unknown, expired,
1802                // already used, or issued to a different client — on a request
1803                // that is NOT a return leg.
1804                //
1805                // Reported to the relying party when the request names a
1806                // `redirect_uri` this client registered, and only then. RFC
1807                // 6749 §4.1.2.1 permits exactly that and nothing wider: the
1808                // target is compared against the registration before anything
1809                // is sent to it, so a browser cannot nominate where a refusal
1810                // goes. It is the same test the `prompt=none` and
1811                // `user_declined` arms of `resolve_authorize_principal` make,
1812                // and the same one the peek makes before the login hop.
1813                //
1814                // The pushed copy's own `redirect_uri` is not available and is
1815                // not what is used: resolving the handle is the thing that just
1816                // failed. This target comes from the query string and survives
1817                // only because the registration vouched for it.
1818                //
1819                // Why redirect at all, when this endpoint deliberately renders
1820                // a page for a refusal it cannot attribute: a relying party
1821                // that is told `invalid_request` can push again and restart,
1822                // while a person looking at a page can only be asked to go back
1823                // to the application and try — which is the worse of the two
1824                // outcomes whenever the better one is available. The page is
1825                // still what an unregistered or absent `redirect_uri` gets,
1826                // which is the case `authorize_error_response` was written for.
1827                //
1828                // A request with no registered target is answered exactly as
1829                // before this change, so the three PAR refusal modules that
1830                // already render a readable page keep doing so.
1831                Err(e) => {
1832                    return refuse_request_uri_to_client(
1833                        &state,
1834                        &http_req,
1835                        user.tenant_id,
1836                        &q.client_id,
1837                        q.redirect_uri.as_deref(),
1838                        q.state.as_deref(),
1839                        &e,
1840                    )
1841                    .await;
1842                }
1843            };
1844
1845            // X7.1 — parsed from the *pushed* copy, never from the query
1846            // string. `state` and `nonce` already work this way and for the
1847            // same reason: a parameter the client chose at push time must not
1848            // be substitutable by the browser that carries the handle.
1849            let authn_params = AuthnRequestParams::parse(&RawAuthnParams::from(&params));
1850
1851            // W4 (T1.3) — and this is the other half of that sentence: the
1852            // query string's copies are not merely ignored, they are grounds
1853            // to refuse the request on the honour lane. Parsed rather than
1854            // tested field by field so "an authentication-request parameter
1855            // arrived" means the same thing here as everywhere else, blank
1856            // template values included.
1857            let inline_authn_params_beside_request_uri =
1858                !AuthnRequestParams::parse(&RawAuthnParams {
1859                    prompt: q.prompt.as_deref(),
1860                    max_age: q.max_age.as_deref(),
1861                    acr_values: q.acr_values.as_deref(),
1862                    claims: q.claims.as_deref(),
1863                    id_token_hint: q.id_token_hint.as_deref(),
1864                    login_hint: q.login_hint.as_deref(),
1865                    display: q.display.as_deref(),
1866                    ui_locales: q.ui_locales.as_deref(),
1867                    claims_locales: q.claims_locales.as_deref(),
1868                    // Not one of the nine, and not counted by `is_empty`
1869                    // either way: a query-string copy beside a `request_uri`
1870                    // is ignored (the pushed copy wins), never refused.
1871                    axiam_idp_hint: None,
1872                })
1873                .is_empty();
1874
1875            AuthorizeRequest {
1876                tenant_id: user.tenant_id,
1877                user_id: user.user_id,
1878                response_type: params.response_type,
1879                client_id: q.client_id,
1880                redirect_uri: params.redirect_uri,
1881                scope: params.scope,
1882                // `state` and `nonce` come from the pushed request too: they
1883                // were the client's to choose at push time, and honouring a
1884                // query-string copy would let the browser substitute its own.
1885                state: params.state,
1886                code_challenge: params.code_challenge,
1887                code_challenge_method: params.code_challenge_method,
1888                nonce: params.nonce,
1889                // B5: the browser session this authorization happens within.
1890                // It reaches the ID token as `sid`, which is what lets a
1891                // back-channel logout token name one session rather than the
1892                // subject's every session.
1893                session_id: Some(user.session_id),
1894                via_par: true,
1895                id_token_hint: decode_authorize_id_token_hint(&state, &authn_params),
1896                inline_authn_params_beside_request_uri,
1897                login_hop_return_leg: axiam_oauth2::login_hop::is_return_leg(
1898                    q.login_hop.as_deref(),
1899                ),
1900                authn_params,
1901                request_object,
1902                session_evidence,
1903                consent_hop_return_leg: q.consent_hop.is_some(),
1904                // RFC 9449 §10 — from the PUSHED copy, never the query string,
1905                // for exactly the reason `state` and `nonce` are just above.
1906                // The PAR endpoint already resolved §10.1's two carriers into
1907                // one value under client authentication; a query-string
1908                // `dpop_jkt` here is a browser proposing a key, which is the
1909                // substitution the binding exists to prevent.
1910                dpop_jkt: params.dpop_jkt,
1911                // OIDC Core §5.5 — from the PUSHED copy, like every other
1912                // parameter beside a `request_uri`.
1913                requested_userinfo_claims: params
1914                    .claims
1915                    .as_deref()
1916                    .map(axiam_oauth2::claims_request::userinfo_claims)
1917                    .unwrap_or_default(),
1918                // RFC 8707 — from the PUSHED copy, like every other parameter
1919                // beside a `request_uri`. The PAR endpoint already validated
1920                // it against this client's allow-list under client
1921                // authentication; a query-string `resource` here is a browser
1922                // proposing an audience, which is the substitution PAR exists
1923                // to prevent.
1924                resource: params.resource,
1925                // Resolved below, once, for both carriers.
1926                sensitive_scopes: axiam_oauth2::sensitive::Requested::None,
1927                sensitive_scopes_switch_is_off: false,
1928                // T21.4 / D4 — a placeholder, resolved below for the same
1929                // reason the two above are: both arms produce the same inputs
1930                // and a copy of the resolution in each is a copy that can
1931                // drift.
1932                external_consent: axiam_oauth2::external_consent::Requested::NotApplicable,
1933            }
1934        }
1935        None => {
1936            let authn_params = AuthnRequestParams::parse(&RawAuthnParams {
1937                prompt: q.prompt.as_deref(),
1938                max_age: q.max_age.as_deref(),
1939                acr_values: q.acr_values.as_deref(),
1940                claims: q.claims.as_deref(),
1941                id_token_hint: q.id_token_hint.as_deref(),
1942                login_hint: q.login_hint.as_deref(),
1943                display: q.display.as_deref(),
1944                ui_locales: q.ui_locales.as_deref(),
1945                claims_locales: q.claims_locales.as_deref(),
1946                axiam_idp_hint: q.axiam_idp_hint.as_deref(),
1947            });
1948            // Cloned into the scrutinee so the else-arm can still read
1949            // `q.redirect_uri`: a `let`-else MOVES what it destructures, and
1950            // the arm's whole job is to decide whether that URI is one this
1951            // server may redirect an error to. Two small `Option<String>`
1952            // clones on a request that is about to be refused.
1953            let (Some(response_type), Some(redirect_uri)) =
1954                (q.response_type.clone(), q.redirect_uri.clone())
1955            else {
1956                // Which of the two is missing decides how the error travels,
1957                // and until this wave it did not: both were answered with a
1958                // JSON body in the browser's window.
1959                //
1960                // RFC 6749 §4.1.2.1 draws the line at whether the server can
1961                // trust where it would be sending the browser. It MUST NOT
1962                // redirect when the `redirect_uri` is missing or does not match
1963                // a registered one — that is the open-redirect case, and a JSON
1964                // body is the right answer. But when `client_id` names a real
1965                // client and the `redirect_uri` is one it registered, the error
1966                // MUST be delivered by redirecting, with `state` echoed, so the
1967                // relying party learns what happened instead of the end user
1968                // reading a machine-readable body they cannot act on.
1969                //
1970                // Found by the OpenID Foundation suite:
1971                // `oidcc-response-type-missing` sends a valid client and a
1972                // registered `redirect_uri` with no `response_type`, waited for
1973                // a redirect that never came, and stalled the whole plan.
1974                // `user.tenant_id`, NOT `q.tenant_id`. The query parameter is
1975                // ignored on this arm for the reason stated where the principal
1976                // was resolved: the tenant a token was minted for is the tenant
1977                // it acts in, and letting a query parameter move it would be a
1978                // tenant-crossing primitive handed to whoever holds the
1979                // browser. Reintroducing it here — on a lookup that decides
1980                // where a browser gets redirected — would be the worst place to
1981                // reintroduce it.
1982                let redirect_target = match &q.redirect_uri {
1983                    Some(candidate) => state
1984                        .oauth2_client_repo
1985                        .get_by_client_id(user.tenant_id, &q.client_id)
1986                        .await
1987                        .ok()
1988                        .filter(|client| any_redirect_uri_matches(&client.redirect_uris, candidate))
1989                        .map(|_| candidate.clone()),
1990                    None => None,
1991                };
1992
1993                // A refused request object with no inline redirect_uri cannot
1994                // be reported by redirecting — there is no validated URI to
1995                // redirect to — so it is answered directly, with its own code
1996                // rather than the generic complaint about missing parameters.
1997                let error = match request_object {
1998                    Some(object) => object.into_error(),
1999                    None => OAuth2Error::InvalidRequest(
2000                        "response_type is required unless request_uri is used".into(),
2001                    ),
2002                };
2003
2004                return match redirect_target {
2005                    Some(uri) => build_error_redirect(
2006                        &uri,
2007                        &error,
2008                        q.state.as_deref(),
2009                        &issuer_config(&http_req, &state),
2010                    ),
2011                    None => authorize_error_response(&http_req, &error),
2012                };
2013            };
2014            AuthorizeRequest {
2015                tenant_id: user.tenant_id,
2016                user_id: user.user_id,
2017                response_type,
2018                client_id: q.client_id,
2019                redirect_uri,
2020                scope: q.scope,
2021                state: q.state,
2022                code_challenge: q.code_challenge,
2023                code_challenge_method: q.code_challenge_method,
2024                nonce: q.nonce,
2025                session_id: Some(user.session_id),
2026                via_par: false,
2027                id_token_hint: decode_authorize_id_token_hint(&state, &authn_params),
2028                // Not a pushed request: there is no second copy to conflict
2029                // with.
2030                inline_authn_params_beside_request_uri: false,
2031                login_hop_return_leg: axiam_oauth2::login_hop::is_return_leg(
2032                    q.login_hop.as_deref(),
2033                ),
2034                authn_params,
2035                request_object,
2036                session_evidence,
2037                consent_hop_return_leg: q.consent_hop.is_some(),
2038                // RFC 9449 §10, Figure 25 — the parameter on a plain
2039                // authorization request. Taken at face value: it is only a
2040                // *commitment*, and the client makes the commitment harder to
2041                // meet, never easier. A caller who pins a key they do not hold
2042                // has locked themselves out of their own code and nobody else.
2043                dpop_jkt: q.dpop_jkt,
2044                // OIDC Core §5.5, from the query string. Parsed rather than
2045                // trusted: `userinfo_claims` filters to the claims AXIAM will
2046                // release on a request alone, so a client naming
2047                // `phone_number` here gets what it would have got before —
2048                // nothing, unless the consent ceremony ran.
2049                requested_userinfo_claims: q
2050                    .claims
2051                    .as_deref()
2052                    .map(axiam_oauth2::claims_request::userinfo_claims)
2053                    .unwrap_or_default(),
2054                // RFC 8707 §2, from the query string. Validated by the
2055                // authorization service against this client's
2056                // `allowed_resources`, after the `redirect_uri` is known good,
2057                // so an `invalid_target` redirects to a URI the client
2058                // registered rather than rendering at AXIAM's own origin.
2059                resource: q.resource.clone(),
2060                // Resolved below, once, for both carriers.
2061                sensitive_scopes: axiam_oauth2::sensitive::Requested::None,
2062                sensitive_scopes_switch_is_off: false,
2063                // T21.4 / D4 — a placeholder, resolved below for the same
2064                // reason the two above are: both arms produce the same inputs
2065                // and a copy of the resolution in each is a copy that can
2066                // drift.
2067                external_consent: axiam_oauth2::external_consent::Requested::NotApplicable,
2068            }
2069        }
2070    };
2071
2072    // W7 — resolved here rather than inside either arm above because both
2073    // arms produce the same three inputs (client, scope, subject) and a copy
2074    // of this in each is a copy that can drift. It is also the first point at
2075    // which the *resolved* scope is known: a pushed request's scope comes from
2076    // the pushed copy, never from the query string.
2077    let mut req = req;
2078    (req.sensitive_scopes, req.sensitive_scopes_switch_is_off) = resolve_sensitive_scopes(
2079        &state,
2080        user.tenant_id,
2081        user.user_id,
2082        &req.client_id,
2083        req.scope.as_deref(),
2084    )
2085    .await;
2086    // T21.4 / D4 — resolved beside W7's, from the same three inputs, and for
2087    // the same reason it is resolved here rather than in either arm above.
2088    req.external_consent = resolve_external_consent(
2089        &state,
2090        user.tenant_id,
2091        user.user_id,
2092        &req.client_id,
2093        req.scope.as_deref(),
2094    )
2095    .await;
2096    let req = req;
2097
2098    // Captured before `req` moves: the error path needs the *resolved*
2099    // redirect_uri and state, which for a PAR request came from the pushed
2100    // parameters rather than the query string.
2101    let resolved_redirect_uri = req.redirect_uri.clone();
2102    let resolved_state = req.state.clone();
2103    let authorized_client_id = req.client_id.clone();
2104
2105    // W4 — was this a `prompt=none` request? Captured before `req` moves so
2106    // the audit rows below can name the outcome of a silent authorization
2107    // (plan §4.2, "Threats and what remains"): the one-bit login-status oracle
2108    // `prompt=none` inherently gives a registered relying party is accepted,
2109    // and made visible.
2110    let silent = req
2111        .authn_params
2112        .prompt
2113        .contains(&axiam_oauth2::authn_params::Prompt::None);
2114
2115    // W5 — the presentation the sign-in page will be asked for, if it is
2116    // shown. Captured before `req` moves, from the bundle whichever carrier
2117    // delivered it, and used **only** inside the interaction arm below.
2118    //
2119    // There is no honour-lane condition here, and W7 is why it now needs
2120    // saying rather than assuming. W4 produced `AuthorizeOutcome::Interact` at
2121    // exactly one place — step 6b, inside `if fapi::honours_authn_params` — so
2122    // a client on the `ignore` lane could not reach the arm that reads this.
2123    // W7 added a second producer, step 6c's consent gate, which is *not*
2124    // lane-conditional: the tenant switch and the consent record decide it,
2125    // not the client's `authn_request_params`.
2126    //
2127    // That is still invariant 4, and for a reason stronger than a lane check:
2128    // reaching step 6c at all requires the request to carry `address` or
2129    // `phone`, and requires the client to have them registered. No client in
2130    // any existing deployment does — the scopes were unregistrable before this
2131    // wave, which is the whole of I4 for G8. A client that has them was
2132    // registered after W7 shipped, by an operator who turned the tenant switch
2133    // on to do it.
2134    //
2135    // `build_interaction_redirect` then decides which page and which of these
2136    // values it may carry; `login_hint` reaches the consent page from neither
2137    // lane.
2138    //
2139    // Assembled here rather than inside `honour::evaluate` because the cosmetic
2140    // four decide no `Outcome` — see that module's "W5's cosmetic four are not
2141    // here".
2142    //
2143    // W7 supplies the tenant default W5 left as `None`, and reads it only when
2144    // the request carried a bundle: an empty bundle selects nothing whatever
2145    // the tenant prefers, so the read would be a database round trip on every
2146    // authorization request in the deployment to reach the same answer.
2147    let cosmetic_owned = {
2148        let tenant_default = if req.authn_params.is_empty() {
2149            None
2150        } else {
2151            tenant_default_locale(&state, user.tenant_id).await
2152        };
2153        let c = axiam_oauth2::login_hop::Cosmetic::from_params(&req.authn_params, tenant_default);
2154        (c.login_hint.map(str::to_owned), c.display, c.ui_locale)
2155    };
2156    // G-B (#817) — the provider hint, from whichever carrier delivered the
2157    // request (the pushed copy for PAR), for a sign-in hop below. Resolved
2158    // only if one is built: with a live session and no interaction there is
2159    // no sign-in page, and the hint is moot.
2160    let idp_hint = req.authn_params.idp_hint.clone();
2161
2162    // T21.6 — see `resolve_authorize_principal`'s use of the same value: an
2163    // interaction hop comes back to the endpoint the request arrived at.
2164    let authorize_path = authorize_path_of(&http_req);
2165
2166    let authorized = state.oauth2.authorize_service.authorize(req).await;
2167    // G-5 (D-53 (1)), after the request is known to be a valid one: the return
2168    // leg of a step-up tells SSF receivers the level changed. A request the
2169    // service refused consumes nothing (F4 W4 P23W4-02).
2170    if return_leg && authorized.is_ok() {
2171        ssf_step_up_return_leg(
2172            &state,
2173            user.tenant_id,
2174            user.user_id,
2175            user.session_id,
2176            session_acr,
2177        )
2178        .await;
2179    }
2180    let outcome = match authorized {
2181        Ok(axiam_oauth2::authorize::AuthorizeOutcome::Interact(interaction)) => {
2182            // W4 — the honour lane asked for an interaction. It rides W3's
2183            // login hop: same `return_to`, same validation on both sides, same
2184            // marker, so the chain is still bounded at one redirect.
2185            //
2186            // `reauth` is set for every reason the *honour lane* interacts —
2187            // `prompt=login`, a `max_age` this session cannot satisfy, a
2188            // step-up, an `id_token_hint` naming somebody else — because each
2189            // is a reason not to trust what the browser already holds; a
2190            // sign-in page that silently reused the current session would
2191            // return the same unsatisfying session and the loop guard would
2192            // have to catch it.
2193            //
2194            // W7 adds the one reason that is not: a consent question. The end
2195            // user is signed in, and what is missing is their answer, not
2196            // their password. `Reason::requires_reauthentication` is where
2197            // that distinction lives, so the two callers of this redirect
2198            // cannot come to disagree about it.
2199            tracing::debug!(
2200                client_id = %authorized_client_id,
2201                reason = ?interaction.reason,
2202                required_acr = ?interaction.required_acr,
2203                "an authorization request on the honour lane needs an interaction"
2204            );
2205            // G-5 (D-53 (1)): a step-up sends the user to raise the level of a
2206            // session they hold. Remember which session and level, server-side,
2207            // so the return leg can tell receivers the level changed.
2208            if interaction.reason == axiam_oauth2::honour::Reason::AcrUnsatisfied
2209                && let Some(acr) = session_acr
2210            {
2211                ssf_step_up_begin(&state, user.tenant_id, user.user_id, user.session_id, acr).await;
2212            }
2213            // W7 — a consent hop marks its own return leg. Same `return_to`
2214            // machinery, same validation on both sides, one more marker; see
2215            // `login_hop::CONSENT_HOP_MARKER` for why sharing the login one
2216            // would answer the consent question with the login hop's evidence.
2217            // T23.1.8: the query as the client sent it — see `client_query_of`.
2218            let client_query = client_query_of(&http_req);
2219            let built = if interaction.reason.requires_reauthentication() {
2220                axiam_oauth2::login_hop::build_return_to_at(&authorize_path, &client_query)
2221            } else {
2222                axiam_oauth2::login_hop::build_consent_return_to_at(&authorize_path, &client_query)
2223            };
2224            let Some(return_to) = built else {
2225                // Nothing safe to come back to, so there is nothing to send
2226                // the browser away for. The relying party is told what is
2227                // missing instead.
2228                return build_error_redirect(
2229                    &resolved_redirect_uri,
2230                    &OAuth2Error::LoginRequired(
2231                        "this authorization request needs the end user to authenticate, and \
2232                         could not be resumed after a sign-in"
2233                            .into(),
2234                    ),
2235                    resolved_state.as_deref(),
2236                    &issuer_config(&http_req, &state),
2237                );
2238            };
2239            if !return_to_is_on_this_deployment(&state, &return_to, &authorize_path) {
2240                return build_error_redirect(
2241                    &resolved_redirect_uri,
2242                    &OAuth2Error::LoginRequired(
2243                        "this authorization request needs the end user to authenticate, and \
2244                         could not be resumed after a sign-in"
2245                            .into(),
2246                    ),
2247                    resolved_state.as_deref(),
2248                    &issuer_config(&http_req, &state),
2249                );
2250            }
2251            let (login_hint, display, ui_locale) = &cosmetic_owned;
2252            // G-B (#817) — a re-authentication hop is a sign-in page too, so
2253            // the hint (or the client's default) applies there exactly as on
2254            // the first leg. The consent page starts no sign-in and is not
2255            // asked: `build_interaction_redirect` drops `idp` for it anyway,
2256            // and skipping the lookup keeps that arm's cost unchanged.
2257            let idp = if interaction.reason.requires_reauthentication() {
2258                let default_idp = state
2259                    .oauth2_client_repo
2260                    .get_by_client_id(user.tenant_id, &authorized_client_id)
2261                    .await
2262                    .ok()
2263                    .and_then(|c| c.default_idp);
2264                resolve_login_idp(&state, user.tenant_id, idp_hint, default_idp).await
2265            } else {
2266                None
2267            };
2268            let cosmetic = axiam_oauth2::login_hop::Cosmetic {
2269                login_hint: login_hint.as_deref(),
2270                display: *display,
2271                ui_locale: *ui_locale,
2272                idp,
2273                // #835 — first leg only: this hop already says `reauth=1` for
2274                // every reason it sends a browser to sign in.
2275                fresh_authentication: false,
2276            };
2277            let location = axiam_oauth2::login_hop::build_interaction_redirect(
2278                &return_to,
2279                interaction.reason,
2280                interaction.required_acr,
2281                &cosmetic,
2282            );
2283            return HttpResponse::Found()
2284                .append_header((actix_web::http::header::LOCATION, location))
2285                .append_header((actix_web::http::header::CACHE_CONTROL, "no-store"))
2286                .append_header(("Referrer-Policy", "no-referrer"))
2287                .finish();
2288        }
2289        other => other.map(|o| match o {
2290            axiam_oauth2::authorize::AuthorizeOutcome::Code(resp) => resp,
2291            axiam_oauth2::authorize::AuthorizeOutcome::Interact(_) => {
2292                unreachable!("the interaction arm returned above")
2293            }
2294        }),
2295    };
2296
2297    if silent {
2298        audit_prompt_none(
2299            &state,
2300            &http_req,
2301            user.tenant_id,
2302            &authorized_client_id,
2303            outcome.as_ref().err(),
2304        )
2305        .await;
2306    }
2307
2308    match outcome {
2309        Ok(resp) => {
2310            // B5: the client has just joined this session. Recorded here —
2311            // the moment a code is issued — because that is when
2312            // participation becomes true, and back-channel logout iterates
2313            // exactly these rows. A failure is logged and swallowed: losing a
2314            // logout notification is bad, but failing the login that would
2315            // have created the session is worse.
2316            if let Err(e) = state
2317                .oauth2
2318                .session_client_repo
2319                .record(axiam_core::models::oauth2_client::CreateSessionClient {
2320                    tenant_id: user.tenant_id,
2321                    session_id: user.session_id,
2322                    client_id: authorized_client_id.clone(),
2323                    user_id: user.user_id,
2324                })
2325                .await
2326            {
2327                tracing::warn!(
2328                    error = %e,
2329                    "could not record session participation; back-channel \
2330                     logout will not reach this client"
2331                );
2332            }
2333
2334            match url::Url::parse(&resp.redirect_uri) {
2335                Ok(mut url) => {
2336                    url.query_pairs_mut().append_pair("code", &resp.code);
2337                    if let Some(ref state) = resp.state {
2338                        url.query_pairs_mut().append_pair("state", state);
2339                    }
2340                    append_issuer(&mut url, &issuer_config(&http_req, &state));
2341                    HttpResponse::Found()
2342                        .append_header(("Location", url.to_string()))
2343                        .finish()
2344                }
2345                Err(_) => {
2346                    // Never leak the authorization code to an
2347                    // unknown host.
2348                    HttpResponse::InternalServerError().json(OAuth2ErrorResponse {
2349                        error: "server_error".into(),
2350                        error_description: "invalid redirect_uri in \
2351                                 authorization response"
2352                            .into(),
2353                    })
2354                }
2355            }
2356        }
2357        Err(e) => {
2358            // Per RFC 6749: only redirect when the redirect_uri has
2359            // been validated. For InvalidClient / redirect_uri
2360            // errors, return a direct HTTP error instead.
2361            match &e {
2362                // B5: `ParRequired` joins these two because it is raised
2363                // BEFORE redirect_uri validation — redirecting would bounce
2364                // the user agent to an unvalidated URI that arrived by the
2365                // very channel the setting forbids.
2366                OAuth2Error::InvalidClient(_)
2367                | OAuth2Error::InvalidRedirectUri(_)
2368                | OAuth2Error::ParRequired(_) => authorize_error_response(&http_req, &e),
2369                _ => {
2370                    // These errors occur after client+redirect_uri
2371                    // were validated — safe to redirect.
2372                    build_error_redirect(
2373                        &resolved_redirect_uri,
2374                        &e,
2375                        resolved_state.as_deref(),
2376                        &issuer_config(&http_req, &state),
2377                    )
2378                }
2379            }
2380        }
2381    }
2382}
2383
2384/// `POST /oauth2/token` -- OAuth2 token endpoint.
2385///
2386/// Accepts form-encoded body per RFC 6749. Supports authorization_code,
2387/// client_credentials, and refresh_token grant types. The `tenant_id` is
2388/// passed as a query parameter since the token endpoint is unauthenticated
2389/// (the client is authenticating itself here).
2390///
2391/// Also serves the native sign-in grant
2392/// `urn:axiam:params:oauth:grant-type:provider-id-token`, which signs a user
2393/// in with an ID token a mobile or desktop app obtained from a federated OIDC
2394/// provider's native SDK. Parameters: `provider` (federation config UUID or
2395/// provider slug), `id_token`, `nonce` (the raw nonce; the provider's SDK was
2396/// given its SHA-256 hex, in either case, which the ID token's `nonce` claim
2397/// must equal), and optional `scope` and `resource`. Every refusal of the provider token is
2398/// `invalid_grant` ("the provider ID token is not acceptable"); the grant is
2399/// rate limited per tenant and client address (`rate_limit_exceeded`). This
2400/// is not token exchange (RFC 8693).
2401#[utoipa::path(
2402    post,
2403    path = "/oauth2/token",
2404    tag = "oauth2",
2405    params(TenantQuery),
2406    request_body(
2407        content_type = "application/x-www-form-urlencoded",
2408        content = TokenRequest,
2409    ),
2410    responses(
2411        (status = 200, description = "Token response",
2412         body = TokenResponse),
2413        (status = 400, description = "OAuth2 error",
2414         body = OAuth2ErrorResponse),
2415        (status = 401, description = "`invalid_client`: client authentication failed; carries a `WWW-Authenticate` challenge",
2416         body = OAuth2ErrorResponse),
2417        (status = 429, description = "`rate_limit_exceeded` from the route limiters and from the provider-id-token grant (with `Retry-After`), or `slow_down` on the uma-ticket and token-exchange grants",
2418         body = OAuth2ErrorResponse),
2419        (status = 500, description = "`server_error`: an internal failure; the body always carries `error_description`",
2420         body = OAuth2ErrorResponse),
2421    ),
2422)]
2423pub async fn token<C: Connection + Clone>(
2424    req: HttpRequest,
2425    tenant_query: web::Query<TenantQuery>,
2426    form: web::Form<TokenRequest>,
2427    state: web::Data<AppState<C>>,
2428) -> HttpResponse {
2429    // W8 / RFC 6749 §5.2 — the challenge is decided from the request,
2430    // before the handler runs, and applied to whatever 401 it produces.
2431    let challenge = client_auth_challenge(&req);
2432    with_client_auth_challenge(token_inner(req, tenant_query, form, state).await, challenge)
2433}
2434
2435/// The token endpoint proper. Split from [`token`] only so that the RFC 6749
2436/// §5.2 challenge is applied to every one of its exits.
2437async fn token_inner<C: Connection + Clone>(
2438    req: HttpRequest,
2439    tenant_query: web::Query<TenantQuery>,
2440    form: web::Form<TokenRequest>,
2441    state: web::Data<AppState<C>>,
2442) -> HttpResponse {
2443    let tenant_id = tenant_query.into_inner().tenant_id;
2444
2445    let form = form.into_inner();
2446    // I5: keep the grant type for the stage-timing event below. The token
2447    // service moves `form`, so copy the (short) discriminator first.
2448    let grant_type = form.grant_type.clone();
2449    // §17.2 residual 3: same reason, for the failed-client-auth audit event
2450    // below. Never the secret — only the client id, which is the caller's own
2451    // input and is what an operator needs to correlate an attack.
2452    let attempted_client_id = form.client_id.clone();
2453    let started = std::time::Instant::now();
2454
2455    // T21.5 — before client authentication, for the reason the authorize
2456    // endpoint calls it before the client lookup: what follows must act on an
2457    // ordinary row. Every grant, the device-code grant included since #635.
2458    if let Some(client_id) = form.client_id.as_deref() {
2459        crate::cimd::materialise_if_cimd(&state, &req, tenant_id, client_id).await;
2460    }
2461
2462    // SEC-096: ONE context construction and ONE DPoP verification, ahead of
2463    // every grant that authenticates a client. Before this, the
2464    // token-exchange and uma-ticket grants each `return`ed above this point,
2465    // so a DPoP proof on those requests was never verified, never recorded as
2466    // single-use, and never available to the binding decision — which meant a
2467    // client holding a `cnf`-bound token could exchange it for a plain bearer
2468    // one, and a `fapi2` client could obtain an unconstrained token from a
2469    // grant `fapi::enforce_token_request` never saw.
2470    //
2471    // #635 (R101W3-01): the device-code grant used to be dispatched above this
2472    // line, on the reading that RFC 8628 authenticates no client. That holds
2473    // for a public client only, and it left a confidential, sender-constrained
2474    // or `fapi2` client's device grant redeemable by anyone holding the device
2475    // code, as an unbound bearer token. It is now dispatched below, with the
2476    // CIBA grant, and authenticates its client like every other grant.
2477    let ctx = match token_request_context(&req) {
2478        Ok(ctx) => ctx.with_assertion_from(&form),
2479        Err(response) => return *response,
2480    };
2481    let ctx = match dpop_from_request(&req, &state, tenant_id, ctx).await {
2482        Ok(ctx) => ctx,
2483        Err(response) => return *response,
2484    };
2485
2486    // There is deliberately no second, per-`(client_id, address)` bucket for a
2487    // request that carries no client credential (#685, D-3). T21.2 added one
2488    // and R110W3-01 re-keyed it; with the route's limiters selecting the same
2489    // address from the same `token_per_min`, it could only refuse a request
2490    // the route had already admitted, never before it — a backstop that added
2491    // no refusal of its own and a second `429` (`slow_down`) a public client
2492    // had to tell apart from the device grant's own. A credential-less flood
2493    // from one address meets the route's `rate_limit_exceeded` first; the
2494    // device poll's `slow_down` is the poll-interval answer alone
2495    // (`poll_device_grant`).
2496
2497    // B3 / RFC 8693. Like the device grant, its own service behind one match
2498    // arm. The exchanging client must be confidential — it is a service, not
2499    // a television, and an exchange is precisely the operation that should be
2500    // attributable.
2501    if grant_type == TOKEN_EXCHANGE_GRANT_TYPE {
2502        // SEC-093: the transport context travels with the grant, so a client
2503        // registered for `tls_client_auth` / `private_key_jwt` is
2504        // authenticated by the credential its registration names rather than
2505        // by the secret it was also, unavoidably, issued.
2506        return handle_token_exchange(tenant_id, form, &state, &ctx).await;
2507    }
2508
2509    // G-A (W2.3) — native sign-in with a provider ID token. Its own arm for
2510    // the reason the token exchange has one: the grant's collaborators (the
2511    // OIDC federation service, the replay store, the consent records) are the
2512    // REST layer's, and the audit record is written here.
2513    if grant_type == PROVIDER_ID_TOKEN_GRANT_TYPE {
2514        return handle_provider_id_token(&req, tenant_id, form, &state, &ctx).await;
2515    }
2516
2517    // X2 / UMA 2.0 §3.3.1. Third grant dispatched here for the same structural
2518    // reason as the other two: the body is deserialized once, so the grant is
2519    // only knowable after `grant_type` has been read out of it.
2520    if grant_type == UMA_TICKET_GRANT_TYPE {
2521        // The authz checker is pulled from the request here rather than taken
2522        // as a handler parameter on purpose. `/oauth2/token` serves five
2523        // grants, and only this one evaluates anything against the RBAC
2524        // engine; making it an extractor would make the checker a hard
2525        // requirement of the whole endpoint, so a deployment (or a test
2526        // harness) that never uses UMA would start answering 500 to ordinary
2527        // token requests because of a dependency none of them use.
2528        let Some(authz) = req.app_data::<crate::authz::AuthzData>().cloned() else {
2529            return build_oauth2_error_response(&OAuth2Error::ServerError(
2530                "the uma-ticket grant requires an authorization checker".into(),
2531            ));
2532        };
2533        // SEC-093, as for the token-exchange grant above.
2534        return handle_uma_ticket(tenant_id, form, &state, &authz, &ctx).await;
2535    }
2536
2537    // G-7 / CIBA Core §10.1. Dispatched here, after the shared context and the
2538    // DPoP verification, so the CIBA grant is
2539    // counted by every limiter this endpoint has (the route's governor and
2540    // shared counter included) and its client-authentication failures reach
2541    // the same `oauth2.client_auth_failed` audit below — the Keycloak 26.7.x
2542    // lesson is a limiter or a lockout that forgets one grant. It shares the
2543    // rest of this arm with the three grants `exchange` serves.
2544    let result = if grant_type == CIBA_GRANT_TYPE {
2545        state
2546            .oauth2
2547            .token_service
2548            .exchange_ciba(tenant_id, form, &ctx, state.oauth2.ciba_service.requests())
2549            .await
2550    } else if grant_type == DEVICE_CODE_GRANT_TYPE {
2551        // B2 / RFC 8628 §3.4, behind the same limiters and the same
2552        // client-authentication audit as the CIBA grant (#635). The normal
2553        // answers of a device's poll — `authorization_pending`, `slow_down` —
2554        // are errors on the wire and reach `build_oauth2_error_response`
2555        // below without being logged or audited as failures.
2556        poll_device_grant(&state, tenant_id, form, &ctx).await
2557    } else {
2558        state
2559            .oauth2
2560            .token_service
2561            .exchange(tenant_id, form, &ctx)
2562            .await
2563    };
2564    match result {
2565        Ok(resp) => {
2566            let exchange_us = started.elapsed().as_micros() as u64;
2567
2568            // I5: serialize explicitly rather than via `.json()` so the
2569            // serialization cost and the exact response body size are
2570            // observable. Body size is the load-bearing number for the
2571            // Nagle/delayed-ACK hypothesis behind the TLS client-credentials
2572            // plateau: the pathology only fires when a response reaches the
2573            // socket as a full segment plus a small trailing fragment, so run 5
2574            // needs to know where this body sits relative to the path MSS
2575            // (1448 bytes on a standard 1500-byte-MTU docker veth) once TLS
2576            // record and HTTP/2 frame overhead are added.
2577            let t_serialize = std::time::Instant::now();
2578            let body = match serde_json::to_vec(&resp) {
2579                Ok(body) => body,
2580                Err(e) => {
2581                    tracing::error!(error = %e, "failed to serialize token response");
2582                    return HttpResponse::InternalServerError().finish();
2583                }
2584            };
2585            let serialize_us = t_serialize.elapsed().as_micros() as u64;
2586
2587            tracing::debug!(
2588                target: "axiam::perf",
2589                stage = "oauth2.token_endpoint",
2590                %grant_type,
2591                exchange_us,
2592                serialize_us,
2593                response_body_bytes = body.len(),
2594                handler_total_us = started.elapsed().as_micros() as u64,
2595                "token endpoint stage timings (I5)"
2596            );
2597
2598            HttpResponse::Ok()
2599                .append_header(("Cache-Control", "no-store"))
2600                .append_header(("Pragma", "no-cache"))
2601                .content_type("application/json")
2602                .body(body)
2603        }
2604        Err(e) => {
2605            if matches!(e, OAuth2Error::InvalidClient(_)) {
2606                // §22.3 residual 1: this used to be awaited, which put a tenant
2607                // read (and, when it succeeded, an insert) on the response path
2608                // — so a failed client auth returned measurably faster for a
2609                // NONEXISTENT tenant than a real one, on an unauthenticated
2610                // endpoint. Detaching it removes the differential at its source
2611                // rather than trying to balance the two branches, and makes the
2612                // "fire-and-forget" this function has always claimed actually
2613                // true. The response below no longer waits on the audit sink at
2614                // all.
2615                let tenant_repo = state.tenant_repo.clone();
2616                let audit_repo = state.audit_repo.clone();
2617                let attempted = attempted_client_id.clone();
2618                let grant = grant_type.clone();
2619                let peer = peer_ip(&req);
2620                let forwarded = client_ip(&req);
2621                actix_web::rt::spawn(async move {
2622                    append_client_auth_failure_audit(
2623                        &tenant_repo,
2624                        &audit_repo,
2625                        tenant_id,
2626                        attempted.as_deref(),
2627                        &grant,
2628                        peer,
2629                        forwarded,
2630                    )
2631                    .await;
2632                });
2633            }
2634            build_oauth2_error_response(&e)
2635        }
2636    }
2637}
2638
2639/// The device-code grant at the token endpoint (B2, RFC 8628 §3.4; #635).
2640///
2641/// Three steps, in an order that keeps the grant out of reach of anybody but
2642/// its client: the `device_code` must be present (decidable from the request
2643/// alone); the client authenticates — by its registered method, with the
2644/// profile's request-time rules and the token binding decided
2645/// (`TokenService::authenticate_device_poll`) — **before the grant is read**;
2646/// and only then is the grant polled, which refuses one issued to another
2647/// client as an unknown code. A caller holding only a leaked device code
2648/// therefore neither spends it nor learns its state.
2649async fn poll_device_grant<C: Connection + Clone>(
2650    state: &AppState<C>,
2651    tenant_id: Uuid,
2652    form: TokenRequest,
2653    ctx: &TokenRequestContext,
2654) -> Result<TokenResponse, OAuth2Error> {
2655    let device_code = form
2656        .device_code
2657        .as_deref()
2658        .filter(|code| !code.is_empty())
2659        .ok_or_else(|| {
2660            OAuth2Error::InvalidRequest("device_code is required for the device_code grant".into())
2661        })?;
2662    let client = state
2663        .oauth2
2664        .token_service
2665        .authenticate_device_poll(tenant_id, &form, ctx)
2666        .await?;
2667    state
2668        .oauth2
2669        .device_authorization_service
2670        .poll_at(
2671            tenant_id,
2672            &client,
2673            device_code,
2674            form.resource.as_deref(),
2675            // T21.6 — the issuer the request arrived under, from the path.
2676            ctx.issuer.as_deref(),
2677        )
2678        .await
2679}
2680
2681/// Build the token endpoint's transport context from the connection (X5.1).
2682///
2683/// The certificate comes from [`VerifiedClientCert`], which `axiam-server`'s
2684/// `on_connect` hook populates from the peer chain **rustls verified during
2685/// the TLS 1.3 handshake**. Nothing else is consulted: in particular the
2686/// `X-Client-Certificate` header that `CertificateAuthenticated` accepts as a
2687/// proxy fallback is deliberately not read here, because an OAuth2 client
2688/// credential must not be assertable by anything that can set a header. See
2689/// `axiam_oauth2::mtls`'s module docs for the full argument.
2690///
2691/// Cost on the ordinary path — a connection with no client certificate, which
2692/// is every non-mTLS deployment — is one `conn_data` lookup that misses and no
2693/// allocation at all. On an mTLS connection it is one copy of the DER plus one
2694/// SHA-256 over it. The X.509 *parse* is deliberately not done here: it is
2695/// deferred to `PresentedCertificate::identity`, which only the
2696/// `tls_client_auth` branch calls, so a deployment running mTLS with ordinary
2697/// secret-authenticating clients does not pay for a DN nobody reads.
2698pub(crate) fn token_request_context(
2699    req: &HttpRequest,
2700) -> Result<TokenRequestContext, Box<HttpResponse>> {
2701    // W8 — RFC 6749 §2.3.1. The header is read here and decoded by
2702    // `axiam_oauth2::client_secret_basic`, so this crate owns "where the bytes
2703    // came from" and that crate owns "what they mean". A malformed header is
2704    // an error rather than an absence, for the same reason a bad DPoP proof is:
2705    // treating it as "no credentials presented" would make a corrupt Basic
2706    // header silently equivalent to sending none.
2707    let basic = basic_credentials_from_request(req)?;
2708
2709    // The trust level travels with the certificate rather than being inferred
2710    // here: `authenticate_mtls_client` refuses a self-asserted certificate for
2711    // `tls_client_auth` (§2.1) and accepts one for `self_signed_tls_client_auth`
2712    // (§2.2), and this is the seam that lets it tell them apart.
2713    let certificate = req.conn_data::<VerifiedClientCert>().map(|verified| {
2714        PresentedCertificate::from_der(&verified.der, verified.trust)
2715            .with_issuer_path(verified.issuer_path.clone())
2716    });
2717
2718    Ok(TokenRequestContext {
2719        client_certificate: certificate,
2720        // T21.6 — the issuer this request arrived under, read from the scope
2721        // middleware's binding and therefore from the PATH, never from the
2722        // form. `None` on `/oauth2/token`, which is every request on a
2723        // deployment with `AXIAM__AUTH__TENANT_ISSUER_PATHS` unset.
2724        issuer: crate::middleware::tenant_path::binding_of(req)
2725            .map(|binding| binding.issuer().to_owned()),
2726        ..TokenRequestContext::default()
2727    }
2728    .with_basic_credentials(basic))
2729}
2730
2731/// Decode an `Authorization: Basic` header, if one is present (W8, RFC 6749
2732/// §2.3.1).
2733///
2734/// `Ok(None)` — no `Authorization` header, or one naming a different scheme.
2735/// Not an error: four of AXIAM's five client-authentication methods put
2736/// nothing in this header.
2737///
2738/// `Err(_)` — the client *did* attempt Basic authentication and got the
2739/// encoding wrong. Answered with `invalid_client`, uniform with every other
2740/// client-authentication failure (SEC-086): the four distinguishable causes in
2741/// [`BasicAuthError`] are a `debug!` for the operator and nothing at all for
2742/// the caller. RFC 6749 §5.2's `WWW-Authenticate: Basic` challenge is attached
2743/// by [`with_client_auth_challenge`] on the way out, so it is impossible for
2744/// one of these endpoints to answer a Basic attempt with a `Bearer` challenge.
2745///
2746/// A second `Authorization` header is refused rather than resolved. Taking the
2747/// first would let an intermediary that appends a header decide which
2748/// credential authenticates — the request-smuggling shape `dpop_from_request`
2749/// refuses for the same reason.
2750fn basic_credentials_from_request(
2751    req: &HttpRequest,
2752) -> Result<Option<BasicCredentials>, Box<HttpResponse>> {
2753    use actix_web::http::header::AUTHORIZATION;
2754
2755    let Some(raw) = req
2756        .headers()
2757        .get(AUTHORIZATION)
2758        .and_then(|v| v.to_str().ok())
2759    else {
2760        return Ok(None);
2761    };
2762
2763    match axiam_oauth2::client_secret_basic::parse_authorization_header(raw) {
2764        // Not a Basic attempt. Nothing is refused here, including a request
2765        // carrying several `Authorization` headers of some other scheme: the
2766        // duplicate-header rule below exists to stop an intermediary choosing
2767        // between two *credentials*, and this endpoint reads no credential
2768        // from any other scheme. Refusing more widely would change the
2769        // behaviour of requests no client sends today, which is I4's whole
2770        // point.
2771        None => Ok(None),
2772        Some(Ok(credentials)) => {
2773            // RFC 9110 §5.2 lets a recipient combine repeated field lines, and
2774            // `Authorization` is not a list-valued field — so two of them is a
2775            // malformed request, not a choice of credentials. Taking the first
2776            // would let an intermediary that appends a header decide which
2777            // credential authenticates, the request-smuggling shape
2778            // `dpop_from_request` refuses for the same reason.
2779            if req.headers().get_all(AUTHORIZATION).count() > 1 {
2780                tracing::debug!(
2781                    "more than one Authorization header on a request attempting Basic client \
2782                     authentication; refusing rather than choosing which credential counts"
2783                );
2784                return Err(Box::new(build_oauth2_error_response(
2785                    &OAuth2Error::InvalidClient(CLIENT_AUTH_FAILED.into()),
2786                )));
2787            }
2788            Ok(Some(credentials))
2789        }
2790        Some(Err(cause)) => {
2791            // The cause, never the header. `BasicAuthError` is four unit
2792            // variants and carries no part of the credential, which is why it
2793            // is safe to log at all.
2794            tracing::debug!(
2795                ?cause,
2796                "an Authorization header using the Basic scheme could not be decoded (RFC 6749 \
2797                 §2.3.1)"
2798            );
2799            Err(Box::new(build_oauth2_error_response(
2800                &OAuth2Error::InvalidClient(CLIENT_AUTH_FAILED.into()),
2801            )))
2802        }
2803    }
2804}
2805
2806/// The `WWW-Authenticate` challenge this request must be answered with if it
2807/// ends in a 401 (RFC 6749 §5.2).
2808///
2809/// The RFC's rule is that the challenge names *the scheme the client used*, so
2810/// it is a property of the request and is computed before the handler runs —
2811/// which is also why it survives every early return inside one.
2812/// `None` means "the client did not authenticate through the `Authorization`
2813/// header", and the endpoint's existing `Bearer realm="axiam"` stands.
2814pub(crate) fn client_auth_challenge(req: &HttpRequest) -> Option<&'static str> {
2815    use actix_web::http::header::AUTHORIZATION;
2816    let raw = req.headers().get(AUTHORIZATION)?.to_str().ok()?;
2817    // Deliberately keyed on the *scheme*, not on whether the credentials
2818    // decoded: a client that sent a corrupt Basic header still used Basic, and
2819    // RFC 6749 §5.2 says the challenge must tell it so.
2820    axiam_oauth2::client_secret_basic::names_basic_scheme(raw)
2821        .then_some(axiam_oauth2::client_secret_basic::BASIC_CHALLENGE)
2822}
2823
2824/// Replace the `WWW-Authenticate` challenge on a 401 with the scheme the
2825/// client actually used (RFC 6749 §5.2).
2826///
2827/// Applied at the four client-authenticating endpoints' single exit rather
2828/// than at each of the thirty-odd `build_oauth2_error_response` call sites
2829/// inside them. That placement is the point: a 401 raised by a path nobody
2830/// thought about while writing this still gets the right challenge.
2831///
2832/// Nothing else about the response is touched, and a non-401 is returned
2833/// unchanged — a challenge on a 200 or a 400 would be a protocol error of its
2834/// own.
2835pub(crate) fn with_client_auth_challenge(
2836    mut resp: HttpResponse,
2837    challenge: Option<&'static str>,
2838) -> HttpResponse {
2839    let Some(challenge) = challenge else {
2840        return resp;
2841    };
2842    if resp.status() != actix_web::http::StatusCode::UNAUTHORIZED {
2843        return resp;
2844    }
2845    resp.headers_mut().insert(
2846        actix_web::http::header::WWW_AUTHENTICATE,
2847        actix_web::http::header::HeaderValue::from_static(challenge),
2848    );
2849    resp
2850}
2851
2852/// The `htu` a DPoP proof must name for this request (SEC-102, RFC 9449 §4.3
2853/// step 9).
2854///
2855/// Built from the **configured** issuer plus the request's path. RFC 9449 says
2856/// `htu` is compared against "the HTTP URI used for the request, without query
2857/// and fragment parts" — meaning the server's own view of its own endpoint.
2858/// `HttpRequest::full_url()` does not give that: it takes the authority from
2859/// `ConnectionInfo`, which prefers `Forwarded`, then `X-Forwarded-Host`, then
2860/// `Host`, and this deployment has no trusted-proxy layer that normalises any
2861/// of the three. With the authority sourced from the request, a caller chooses
2862/// *both* sides of the comparison and the check degrades to "the proof names
2863/// some authority consistently" — so a client phished into signing a proof for
2864/// `https://attacker.example/oauth2/token` could have it accepted here by
2865/// sending a matching `Host`.
2866///
2867/// The path still comes from the request, and must: it is what keeps a
2868/// `/oauth2/par` proof from being replayed at `/oauth2/token`. `req.path()` is
2869/// actix's already-normalised path and carries neither query nor fragment,
2870/// which is exactly the string the RFC compares.
2871///
2872/// `effective_issuer()` is trimmed of any trailing slash by `AuthConfig`, and
2873/// `req.path()` always begins with one, so the join never doubles it.
2874fn dpop_htu<C: Connection + Clone>(state: &AppState<C>, req: &HttpRequest) -> String {
2875    format!("{}{}", state.auth_config.effective_issuer(), req.path())
2876}
2877
2878/// The header a DPoP proof arrives in (RFC 9449 §4).
2879const DPOP_HEADER: &str = "DPoP";
2880
2881/// The response header carrying a nonce challenge (RFC 9449 §8).
2882const DPOP_NONCE_HEADER: &str = "DPoP-Nonce";
2883
2884/// Fold a verified `DPoP` proof into the token-endpoint context (X5.1).
2885///
2886/// All of the verification is [`verify_dpop_header`]; this is the one line of
2887/// token-endpoint-specific work, kept separate so that the PAR endpoint —
2888/// which needs the proof's thumbprint but has no `TokenRequestContext` to put
2889/// it in — reaches the same verification rather than a second copy of it.
2890async fn dpop_from_request<C: Connection + Clone>(
2891    req: &HttpRequest,
2892    state: &AppState<C>,
2893    tenant_id: Uuid,
2894    mut ctx: TokenRequestContext,
2895) -> Result<TokenRequestContext, Box<HttpResponse>> {
2896    let Some(verified) = verify_dpop_header(req, state, tenant_id).await? else {
2897        return Ok(ctx);
2898    };
2899    ctx.dpop_proof = Some(verified);
2900    Ok(ctx)
2901}
2902
2903/// Verify the `DPoP` header, if one is present (X5.1, RFC 9449 §4.3).
2904///
2905/// `Ok(None)` means no header arrived. `Err` means one arrived and did not
2906/// verify, and carries the response to send.
2907///
2908/// Two properties this function exists to hold, both of which are easy to lose
2909/// by writing the obvious thing instead:
2910///
2911/// 1. **A proof that fails verification is an error, not an absence.** Returning
2912///    `Ok(None)` for a *bad* proof would be silently equivalent to not sending
2913///    one — so a client that sent a forged proof would get whatever an unbound
2914///    client gets. The `Err` arm is what stops that.
2915/// 2. **An absent header is not an error here.** Whether this particular client
2916///    needed a proof is `fapi::enforce_token_request`'s question and
2917///    `certificate_binding_for`'s, both of which read the *registration*. Making
2918///    the decision here would require loading the client twice, and would put
2919///    the "does this client need a proof" rule in two places.
2920///
2921/// The `jti` is recorded on success, through the same `UNIQUE`-index guard the
2922/// client-assertion path uses. A proof whose `jti` cannot be recorded is
2923/// refused rather than accepted: failing open would turn a database blip into
2924/// an unlimited replay window.
2925///
2926/// Shared with `/oauth2/par` since RFC 9449 §10.1, which requires the PAR
2927/// endpoint to check a proof "as defined in Section 4.3" — the same check,
2928/// with `htu` naming the PAR path because [`dpop_htu`] reads it from the
2929/// request.
2930async fn verify_dpop_header<C: Connection + Clone>(
2931    req: &HttpRequest,
2932    state: &AppState<C>,
2933    tenant_id: Uuid,
2934) -> Result<Option<axiam_oauth2::dpop::VerifiedDpopProof>, Box<HttpResponse>> {
2935    use axiam_core::repository::{ProofKind, ProofReplayRepository};
2936    use axiam_oauth2::dpop::{self, DpopExpectation};
2937
2938    let Some(raw) = req
2939        .headers()
2940        .get(DPOP_HEADER)
2941        .and_then(|v| v.to_str().ok())
2942        .map(str::trim)
2943        .filter(|v| !v.is_empty())
2944    else {
2945        return Ok(None);
2946    };
2947
2948    // RFC 9449 §4.3 step 2: more than one DPoP header is a malformed request,
2949    // not a choice of proofs. Refusing rather than taking the first is what
2950    // stops a request-smuggling intermediary from deciding which proof counts.
2951    if req.headers().get_all(DPOP_HEADER).count() > 1 {
2952        return Err(dpop_error_response(
2953            "invalid_dpop_proof",
2954            "a request may carry at most one DPoP header",
2955            None,
2956        ));
2957    }
2958
2959    // SEC-102: the authority comes from the CONFIGURED issuer, never from the
2960    // request. `HttpRequest::full_url()` builds it from `ConnectionInfo`,
2961    // which prefers `Forwarded`, then `X-Forwarded-Host`, then `Host` — all
2962    // three request-controlled. Sourcing it there let the caller choose both
2963    // sides of RFC 9449 §4.3 step 9's comparison, reducing the `htu` check to
2964    // "the proof names *some* authority consistently". Only the path is taken
2965    // from the request, and only `req.path()`, which actix has already
2966    // normalised and which carries no query or fragment — exactly what the
2967    // RFC compares against.
2968    let htu = dpop_htu(state, req);
2969    let expect = DpopExpectation {
2970        htm: req.method().as_str(),
2971        htu: &htu,
2972        // SEC-097: `expected_nonce` stays `None` because this deployment
2973        // stores no per-client nonce to compare against — see
2974        // `dpop_nonce_required_for`, which is what decides whether a nonce is
2975        // demanded at all, and `docs/security-profiles.md` for what v1's
2976        // nonce challenge does and does not prove.
2977        expected_nonce: None,
2978        require_nonce: false,
2979        access_token: None,
2980        now: chrono::Utc::now().timestamp(),
2981        max_age_secs: dpop::DEFAULT_PROOF_MAX_AGE_SECS,
2982    };
2983
2984    let verified = match dpop::verify_dpop_proof(raw, &expect) {
2985        Ok(v) => v,
2986        Err(e) if e.is_nonce_challenge() => {
2987            return Err(dpop_error_response(
2988                e.error_code(),
2989                "a DPoP nonce is required",
2990                Some(dpop::new_nonce()),
2991            ));
2992        }
2993        Err(e) => {
2994            tracing::debug!(error = %e, "DPoP proof verification failed at the token endpoint");
2995            return Err(dpop_error_response(
2996                e.error_code(),
2997                "the DPoP proof could not be verified",
2998                None,
2999            ));
3000        }
3001    };
3002
3003    match state
3004        .oauth2
3005        .proof_replay_repo
3006        .insert_proof_jti(
3007            tenant_id,
3008            ProofKind::DpopProof,
3009            &verified.jkt,
3010            &verified.jti,
3011            verified.replay_expiry(dpop::DEFAULT_PROOF_MAX_AGE_SECS),
3012        )
3013        .await
3014    {
3015        Ok(()) => {}
3016        Err(axiam_core::error::AxiamError::ReplayDetected) => {
3017            tracing::warn!(jkt = %verified.jkt, "a DPoP proof was replayed; refusing");
3018            return Err(dpop_error_response(
3019                "invalid_dpop_proof",
3020                "this DPoP proof has already been used",
3021                None,
3022            ));
3023        }
3024        Err(e) => {
3025            tracing::error!(
3026                error = %e,
3027                "could not record a DPoP proof's jti; refusing rather than accepting a proof \
3028                 that cannot be made single-use"
3029            );
3030            return Err(dpop_error_response(
3031                "invalid_dpop_proof",
3032                "the DPoP proof could not be verified",
3033                None,
3034            ));
3035        }
3036    }
3037
3038    Ok(Some(verified))
3039}
3040
3041/// RFC 9449 §7.1 error response, optionally carrying a nonce challenge.
3042///
3043/// Boxed because it is only ever the `Err` of `dpop_from_request`, whose `Ok`
3044/// is a handful of words: an inline `HttpResponse` there makes every caller
3045/// carry the error's width on the success path (`clippy::result_large_err`).
3046fn dpop_error_response(error: &str, description: &str, nonce: Option<String>) -> Box<HttpResponse> {
3047    let mut builder = HttpResponse::BadRequest();
3048    builder
3049        .append_header(("Cache-Control", "no-store"))
3050        .append_header(("Pragma", "no-cache"));
3051    if let Some(nonce) = nonce {
3052        builder.append_header((DPOP_NONCE_HEADER, nonce));
3053    }
3054    Box::new(builder.json(serde_json::json!({
3055        "error": error,
3056        // Through the same renderer `OAuth2Error::error_description` uses.
3057        // This path builds its body directly rather than from an
3058        // `OAuth2Error`, and RFC 6749 §5.2's character set binds the *field*,
3059        // not the type that happened to produce it — a §-carrying description
3060        // written here would be exactly the defect fixed there.
3061        "error_description": axiam_oauth2::error::nqschar(description),
3062    })))
3063}
3064
3065/// Append an `oauth2.client_auth_failed` audit entry, fire-and-forget.
3066///
3067/// §17.2 residual 3: a failed client authentication at the token endpoint used
3068/// to accrue **nothing** — no counter, no lockout, no audit row, no metric —
3069/// and `/oauth2` is not covered by the audit middleware, so nothing else
3070/// filled the gap either. Contrast the interactive login path, which records
3071/// every failure and applies exponential backoff.
3072///
3073/// This closes the **detection** half only, which is the half that was
3074/// genuinely missing. Lockout is deliberately *not* added here: client
3075/// credentials are 256-bit CSPRNG values, so online guessing is not the threat
3076/// — and a lockout keyed on a caller-supplied `client_id` would hand any
3077/// unauthenticated party a denial-of-service against a known client. The
3078/// endpoint's existing rate limit remains the volumetric control.
3079///
3080/// Recorded with `actor_id = Uuid::nil()` and [`ActorType::System`] because
3081/// the caller is by definition unauthenticated: the presented `client_id`
3082/// either does not exist or did not prove possession of its secret, so it must
3083/// not be promoted into the actor field as though it had. It goes in the
3084/// metadata instead, where it is evidence rather than identity.
3085///
3086/// The secret is never recorded, in any form.
3087///
3088/// # SEC-087 — this endpoint is unauthenticated, so nothing here may be trusted
3089///
3090/// `/oauth2/token` takes its `tenant_id` from a **query parameter** and requires
3091/// no credential to reach. The first version of this function wrote the row
3092/// unconditionally, which let any anonymous caller append rows into an arbitrary
3093/// tenant's append-only log — or into a tenant id that never existed — at
3094/// request rate. Three separate controls apply here as a result:
3095///
3096/// 1. **The tenant is resolved before the write**, and a row that cannot be
3097///    attributed to a real tenant goes to the **system partition**
3098///    (`tenant_id = nil`, the one [`AuditLogRepository::list_system`] serves)
3099///    rather than to a caller-chosen id. This bounds what an anonymous caller
3100///    can steer: they may cause a row, but never choose which real tenant's
3101///    append-only log it lands in. It does *not* stop a caller from generating
3102///    failures against a real tenant they do not belong to — and deliberately
3103///    doesn't try to, because that event is **true**: a failed client
3104///    authentication did occur against that tenant, and suppressing it would
3105///    blind the operator to a real attack. The volumetric control is the
3106///    endpoint's rate limit, not the audit layer.
3107///
3108///    §22.3 residual 2: an earlier version *dropped* the row whenever the
3109///    tenant read failed for any reason other than `NotFound`. The reasoning
3110///    (a DB outage must not turn a 401 into a 500) was right, but the effect
3111///    was that a database fault cost exactly the telemetry this row exists to
3112///    provide — and a database fault is plausibly correlated with an incident.
3113///    Routing to the system partition keeps the signal in precisely the case
3114///    where losing it hurts most, while still refusing to guess a tenant.
3115/// 2. **The client id is truncated on a character boundary, by bytes.**
3116///    §22.3 residual 3: the cap was applied with `chars().take(N)`, so a
3117///    multibyte id still stored up to 4N bytes. It is caller-supplied and
3118///    otherwise unbounded, unlike the `client_ip`/`user_agent` helpers which
3119///    have always capped their inputs (and which share the same char-vs-byte
3120///    looseness, bounded there by much smaller limits).
3121/// 3. **The IP is recorded at two trust levels.** `ip_address` carries the
3122///    transport peer address, which a caller cannot forge. The
3123///    `X-Forwarded-For`-derived value goes to metadata under a name that says
3124///    it is untrusted: on an authenticated path behind a trusted proxy the
3125///    forwarded value is the useful one, but here anyone can assert it (when
3126///    `TRUSTED_PROXIES` is empty, every peer's `X-Forwarded-For` is read; with
3127///    it set, only a listed proxy's line is honoured), and an operator who
3128///    blocks a forged IP has been made to act against the wrong host.
3129pub(crate) async fn append_client_auth_failure_audit<C: Connection + Clone>(
3130    tenant_repo: &SurrealTenantRepository<C>,
3131    audit_repo: &SurrealAuditLogRepository<C>,
3132    tenant_id: Uuid,
